Yeah, hi. Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been really stuck in this loop of beating myself up. It’s mostly about work, or at least that’s where it shows up the loudest. I keep thinking I’m not really “senior” material, like I somehow talked my way into people believing I can handle more responsibility than I actually can.

And it’s not just a passing thought, you know? I’ll sit there replaying things I said in meetings or decisions I made, and I end up feeling like I fooled everyone. Like they’re going to realize I’m not as capable as they thought. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it’s been weighing on me enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone instead of just keeping it in my head.

Like everything is still happening, I’m still there, I’m still doing the basic things, but there’s this dullness over it. Even when nothing specific is wrong in that exact moment, I still feel weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think there was a pretty clear turning point. It was about a month ago, maybe a little over or under, when I got asked to cover the senior analyst duties.\n\nAt first I tried to tell myself it was just temporary and I should be able to handle it, but that’s when I really started noticing this whole up-and-down pattern more. Like before that, I’d have stressful days, sure, but it didn’t feel as… noticeable or consuming. Once I was put in that role, it felt like everything started getting louder in my head, especially the pressure and the second-guessing.\n\n[Last Question]\nIf you were no longer covering the senior analyst duties, do you think this heavy low mood would mostly go away?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I don’t think it would mostly go away, no. I mean, I do think the senior analyst stuff is making it louder, definitely. It gives my brain a lot to latch onto. But the low feeling has sort of spread past just that.

I’ll sit there replaying things I said in meetings or decisions I made, and I end up feeling like I fooled everyone. Like they’re going to realize I’m not as capable as they thought. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it’s been weighing on me enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone instead of just keeping it in my head.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you also feel down most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, outside of work it doesn’t just magically turn off when I close the laptop or leave the office. Most days lately there’s this kind of heavy, low feeling that follows me around.\n\nIt’s hard to describe exactly. It’s not always like I’m actively crying or anything that obvious. It’s more like I’m moving through the day at work and then at home with the color turned down, if that makes sense. Like everything is still happening, I’m still there, I’m still doing the basic things, but there’s this dullness over it. Even when nothing specific is wrong in that exact moment, I still feel weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think there was a pretty clear turning point. It was about a month ago, maybe a little over or under, when I got asked to cover the senior analyst duties.\n\nAt first I tried to tell myself it was just temporary and I should be able to handle it, but that’s when I really started noticing this whole up-and-down pattern more. Like before that, I’d have stressful days, sure, but it didn’t feel as… noticeable or consuming. Once I was put in that role, it felt like everything started getting louder in my head, especially the pressure and the second-guessing.\n\nDoctor: If you were no longer covering the senior analyst duties, do you think this heavy low mood would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would mostly go away, no. I mean, I do think the senior analyst stuff is making it louder, definitely. It gives my brain a lot to latch onto. But the low feeling has sort of spread past just that.\n\nLike, even when I’m not actively dealing with work, I still have this heaviness most days lately. I’ll be at home doing normal things, or just trying to get through the evening, and it’s like everything is muted. That’s the best way I can put it — like the color’s been turned down. So if I wasn’t covering those duties, maybe I’d feel less pressure, but I don’t think I’d suddenly feel fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this low most days for at least the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It’s been long enough that it feels kind of embarrassing that I didn’t notice it clearly sooner, but not that long.\n\nLooking back, I’d say these low periods — and especially the guilty feeling that comes with them — have been around off and on for maybe about eleven months. It hasn’t been one perfectly straight line every single day for eleven months, but when I think back, that pattern has been there for most of the past year. It’s only gotten more obvious recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things much less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s actually one of the things that’s been bothering me, because I’m usually pretty simple that way. Like, I don’t need some big exciting thing to look forward to. Normally, if it’s the weekend, I’ll think about grilling something, or maybe going fishing if the weather’s decent, or just putting on a record and messing around in the garage or the house.\n\nBut lately I’ll have the stuff right there — the grill, the fishing gear, my records — and I just kind of… leave it. I’ll think, “I should do that,” because I know I usually like it, but then I don’t really care enough to start. It’s not even that I’m choosing something better instead. It’s more like the interest just isn’t there the way it normally is.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying this out loud, but yes, sort of. I haven’t made any kind of plan to hurt myself, and I don’t have some specific thing I’m intending to do. It’s not like I’m sitting there working out details or anything like that.\n\nBut I do catch myself having thoughts like… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up one morning. Not in a dramatic way, exactly. More like I’m so tired of feeling heavy and guilty and worn down that the idea of not having to keep doing it crosses my mind. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, sleep has been kind of a mess lately. Not every single night in the exact same way, but enough that I notice it.

Some mornings I wake up way too early — like before I actually need to be up — and my brain just starts going straight into work stuff. Client tickets, things I didn’t finish, little problems I need to troubleshoot… and once that starts, I can’t really get back to sleep. I’ll just lie there turning it over in my head, even if there’s nothing I can actually do about it at that hour.

Other times, I technically sleep enough hours, or at least it looks that way if I count them, but I still wake up feeling worn out. Like the sleep didn’t really do what it was supposed to do. It’s frustrating, because I can’t always point to one thing and say, “That’s why I’m tired,” but I feel drained anyway.

I mean, I do think the senior analyst stuff is making it louder, definitely. It gives my brain a lot to latch onto. But the low feeling has sort of spread past just that.\n\nLike, even when I’m not actively dealing with work, I still have this heaviness most days lately. I’ll be at home doing normal things, or just trying to get through the evening, and it’s like everything is muted. That’s the best way I can put it — like the color’s been turned down. So if I wasn’t covering those duties, maybe I’d feel less pressure, but I don’t think I’d suddenly feel fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this low most days for at least the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It’s been long enough that it feels kind of embarrassing that I didn’t notice it clearly sooner, but not that long.\n\nLooking back, I’d say these low periods — and especially the guilty feeling that comes with them — have been around off and on for maybe about eleven months. It hasn’t been one perfectly straight line every single day for eleven months, but when I think back, that pattern has been there for most of the past year. It’s only gotten more obvious recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things much less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s actually one of the things that’s been bothering me, because I’m usually pretty simple that way. Like, I don’t need some big exciting thing to look forward to. Normally, if it’s the weekend, I’ll think about grilling something, or maybe going fishing if the weather’s decent, or just putting on a record and messing around in the garage or the house.\n\nBut lately I’ll have the stuff right there — the grill, the fishing gear, my records — and I just kind of… leave it. I’ll think, “I should do that,” because I know I usually like it, but then I don’t really care enough to start. It’s not even that I’m choosing something better instead. It’s more like the interest just isn’t there the way it normally is.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying this out loud, but yes, sort of. I haven’t made any kind of plan to hurt myself, and I don’t have some specific thing I’m intending to do. It’s not like I’m sitting there working out details or anything like that.\n\nBut I do catch myself having thoughts like… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up one morning. Not in a dramatic way, exactly. More like I’m so tired of feeling heavy and guilty and worn down that the idea of not having to keep doing it crosses my mind. Then I feel bad for even thinking that, because I know how that sounds.\n\nSo… no plan, and I’m not trying to hurt myself, but those thoughts about not waking up have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep has been kind of a mess lately. Not every single night in the exact same way, but enough that I notice it.\n\nSome mornings I wake up way too early — like before I actually need to be up — and my brain just starts going straight into work stuff. Client tickets, things I didn’t finish, little problems I need to troubleshoot… and once that starts, I can’t really get back to sleep. I’ll just lie there turning it over in my head, even if there’s nothing I can actually do about it at that hour.\n\nOther times, I technically sleep enough hours, or at least it looks that way if I count them, but I still wake up feeling worn out. It was about a month ago, maybe a little over or under, when I got asked to cover the senior analyst duties.\n\nAt first I tried to tell myself it was just temporary and I should be able to handle it, but that’s when I really started noticing this whole up-and-down pattern more. Like before that, I’d have stressful days, sure, but it didn’t feel as… noticeable or consuming. Once I was put in that role, it felt like everything started getting louder in my head, especially the pressure and the second-guessing.\n\nDoctor: If you were no longer covering the senior analyst duties, do you think this heavy low mood would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would mostly go away, no. I mean, I do think the senior analyst stuff is making it louder, definitely. It gives my brain a lot to latch onto. But the low feeling has sort of spread past just that.\n\nLike, even when I’m not actively dealing with work, I still have this heaviness most days lately. I’ll be at home doing normal things, or just trying to get through the evening, and it’s like everything is muted. That’s the best way I can put it — like the color’s been turned down. So if I wasn’t covering those duties, maybe I’d feel less pressure, but I don’t think I’d suddenly feel fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this low most days for at least the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It’s been long enough that it feels kind of embarrassing that I didn’t notice it clearly sooner, but not that long.\n\nLooking back, I’d say these low periods — and especially the guilty feeling that comes with them — have been around off and on for maybe about eleven months. It hasn’t been one perfectly straight line every single day for eleven months, but when I think back, that pattern has been there for most of the past year. It’s only gotten more obvious recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things much less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s actually one of the things that’s been bothering me, because I’m usually pretty simple that way. Like, I don’t need some big exciting thing to look forward to. Normally, if it’s the weekend, I’ll think about grilling something, or maybe going fishing if the weather’s decent, or just putting on a record and messing around in the garage or the house.\n\nBut lately I’ll have the stuff right there — the grill, the fishing gear, my records — and I just kind of… leave it. I’ll think, “I should do that,” because I know I usually like it, but then I don’t really care enough to start. It’s not even that I’m choosing something better instead. It’s more like the interest just isn’t there the way it normally is.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying this out loud, but yes, sort of. I haven’t made any kind of plan to hurt myself, and I don’t have some specific thing I’m intending to do. It’s not like I’m sitting there working out details or anything like that.\n\nBut I do catch myself having thoughts like… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up one morning. Not in a dramatic way, exactly. More like I’m so tired of feeling heavy and guilty and worn down that the idea of not having to keep doing it crosses my mind. Then I feel bad for even thinking that, because I know how that sounds.\n\nSo… no plan, and I’m not trying to hurt myself, but those thoughts about not waking up have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep has been kind of a mess lately. Not every single night in the exact same way, but enough that I notice it.\n\nSome mornings I wake up way too early — like before I actually need to be up — and my brain just starts going straight into work stuff. Client tickets, things I didn’t finish, little problems I need to troubleshoot… and once that starts, I can’t really get back to sleep. I’ll just lie there turning it over in my head, even if there’s nothing I can actually do about it at that hour.\n\nOther times, I technically sleep enough hours, or at least it looks that way if I count them, but I still wake up feeling worn out. Like the sleep didn’t really do what it was supposed to do. It’s frustrating, because I can’t always point to one thing and say, “That’s why I’m tired,” but I feel drained anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean… I haven’t noticed it in the same clear way as some of the other stuff. With concentration, it’s a little hard for me to separate normal distraction from an actual problem, because my job kind of forces me to jump between things anyway — emails, tickets, someone asking a question, then back to whatever I was doing.\n\nThere are times I feel like I’m not as sharp as I’d like, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m having real trouble concentrating. I haven’t been keeping track of it or anything. If I’m tired or stressed, then yeah, I might have to reread something or take a minute to get started, but I can’t say for sure that it’s been a major thing lately.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like before that, I’d have stressful days, sure, but it didn’t feel as… noticeable or consuming. Once I was put in that role, it felt like everything started getting louder in my head, especially the pressure and the second-guessing.\n\nDoctor: If you were no longer covering the senior analyst duties, do you think this heavy low mood would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would mostly go away, no. I mean, I do think the senior analyst stuff is making it louder, definitely. It gives my brain a lot to latch onto. But the low feeling has sort of spread past just that.\n\nLike, even when I’m not actively dealing with work, I still have this heaviness most days lately. I’ll be at home doing normal things, or just trying to get through the evening, and it’s like everything is muted. That’s the best way I can put it — like the color’s been turned down. So if I wasn’t covering those duties, maybe I’d feel less pressure, but I don’t think I’d suddenly feel fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this low most days for at least the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It’s been long enough that it feels kind of embarrassing that I didn’t notice it clearly sooner, but not that long.\n\nLooking back, I’d say these low periods — and especially the guilty feeling that comes with them — have been around off and on for maybe about eleven months. It’s only gotten more obvious recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things much less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s actually one of the things that’s been bothering me, because I’m usually pretty simple that way. Like, I don’t need some big exciting thing to look forward to. Normally, if it’s the weekend, I’ll think about grilling something, or maybe going fishing if the weather’s decent, or just putting on a record and messing around in the garage or the house.\n\nBut lately I’ll have the stuff right there — the grill, the fishing gear, my records — and I just kind of… leave it. I’ll think, “I should do that,” because I know I usually like it, but then I don’t really care enough to start. It’s not even that I’m choosing something better instead. It’s more like the interest just isn’t there the way it normally is.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying this out loud, but yes, sort of. Client tickets, things I didn’t finish, little problems I need to troubleshoot… and once that starts, I can’t really get back to sleep. I’ll just lie there turning it over in my head, even if there’s nothing I can actually do about it at that hour.\n\nOther times, I technically sleep enough hours, or at least it looks that way if I count them, but I still wake up feeling worn out. Like the sleep didn’t really do what it was supposed to do. I haven’t been keeping track of it or anything. If I’m tired or stressed, then yeah, I might have to reread something or take a minute to get started, but I can’t say for sure that it’s been a major thing lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t know if I’d say “a lot of different things,” exactly. Work is definitely the thing that grabs my brain the most, just because it’s always there and there’s always something unresolved.

Outside of that… I mean, I worry about normal stuff, I guess. Bills, the house, little family things, whether I forgot to do something, that kind of everyday background noise. But I’m not sure it feels excessive across the board. It’s more like when I’m already worn out, even small things can feel louder than they should.

So… I guess my answer is maybe, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as worrying about everything. It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of disasters all day. It’s more that certain things get stuck in my head, mostly work-related, and then the rest depends on the day.

Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic way, like I’m not refusing food or anything, but my appetite’s definitely been off.

It was about a month ago, maybe a little over or under, when I got asked to cover the senior analyst duties.\n\nAt first I tried to tell myself it was just temporary and I should be able to handle it, but that’s when I really started noticing this whole up-and-down pattern more. Like before that, I’d have stressful days, sure, but it didn’t feel as… noticeable or consuming. Once I was put in that role, it felt like everything started getting louder in my head, especially the pressure and the second-guessing.\n\nDoctor: If you were no longer covering the senior analyst duties, do you think this heavy low mood would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would mostly go away, no. I mean, I do think the senior analyst stuff is making it louder, definitely. It gives my brain a lot to latch onto. But the low feeling has sort of spread past just that.\n\nLike, even when I’m not actively dealing with work, I still have this heaviness most days lately. I’ll be at home doing normal things, or just trying to get through the evening, and it’s like everything is muted. That’s the best way I can put it — like the color’s been turned down. So if I wasn’t covering those duties, maybe I’d feel less pressure, but I don’t think I’d suddenly feel fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this low most days for at least the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It’s been long enough that it feels kind of embarrassing that I didn’t notice it clearly sooner, but not that long.\n\nLooking back, I’d say these low periods — and especially the guilty feeling that comes with them — have been around off and on for maybe about eleven months. It hasn’t been one perfectly straight line every single day for eleven months, but when I think back, that pattern has been there for most of the past year. It’s only gotten more obvious recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things much less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s actually one of the things that’s been bothering me, because I’m usually pretty simple that way. Like, I don’t need some big exciting thing to look forward to. Normally, if it’s the weekend, I’ll think about grilling something, or maybe going fishing if the weather’s decent, or just putting on a record and messing around in the garage or the house.\n\nBut lately I’ll have the stuff right there — the grill, the fishing gear, my records — and I just kind of… leave it. I’ll think, “I should do that,” because I know I usually like it, but then I don’t really care enough to start. It’s not even that I’m choosing something better instead. It’s more like the interest just isn’t there the way it normally is.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying this out loud, but yes, sort of. I haven’t made any kind of plan to hurt myself, and I don’t have some specific thing I’m intending to do. It’s not like I’m sitting there working out details or anything like that.\n\nBut I do catch myself having thoughts like… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up one morning. Not in a dramatic way, exactly. More like I’m so tired of feeling heavy and guilty and worn down that the idea of not having to keep doing it crosses my mind. Then I feel bad for even thinking that, because I know how that sounds.\n\nSo… no plan, and I’m not trying to hurt myself, but those thoughts about not waking up have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep has been kind of a mess lately. Not every single night in the exact same way, but enough that I notice it.\n\nSome mornings I wake up way too early — like before I actually need to be up — and my brain just starts going straight into work stuff. Client tickets, things I didn’t finish, little problems I need to troubleshoot… and once that starts, I can’t really get back to sleep. I’ll just lie there turning it over in my head, even if there’s nothing I can actually do about it at that hour.\n\nOther times, I technically sleep enough hours, or at least it looks that way if I count them, but I still wake up feeling worn out. Like the sleep didn’t really do what it was supposed to do. It’s frustrating, because I can’t always point to one thing and say, “That’s why I’m tired,” but I feel drained anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean… I haven’t noticed it in the same clear way as some of the other stuff. With concentration, it’s a little hard for me to separate normal distraction from an actual problem, because my job kind of forces me to jump between things anyway — emails, tickets, someone asking a question, then back to whatever I was doing.\n\nThere are times I feel like I’m not as sharp as I’d like, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m having real trouble concentrating. I haven’t been keeping track of it or anything. If I’m tired or stressed, then yeah, I might have to reread something or take a minute to get started, but I can’t say for sure that it’s been a major thing lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like I’m collapsing or anything, but I feel worn down in this really annoying, everyday kind of way.\n\nBy the time I get home from the office, I’m just… done. And I don’t mean after doing anything especially intense, just a normal workday. I’ll get home and even stupid little things feel bigger than they should. Like checking the mail, or loading the dishwasher, or dealing with something that would normally take five minutes — I’ll look at it and feel like it’s this whole project.\n\nIt makes me feel kind of ridiculous, honestly, because I know those aren’t hard tasks. But my body and my brain both feel like they’ve already used up whatever energy I had for the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things, not just work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “a lot of different things,” exactly. Work is definitely the thing that grabs my brain the most, just because it’s always there and there’s always something unresolved.\n\nOutside of that… I mean, I worry about normal stuff, I guess. Bills, the house, little family things, whether I forgot to do something, that kind of everyday background noise. But I’m not sure it feels excessive across the board. It’s more like when I’m already worn out, even small things can feel louder than they should.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is maybe, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as worrying about everything. It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of disasters all day. It’s more that certain things get stuck in my head, mostly work-related, and then the rest depends on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been eating noticeably less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic way, like I’m not refusing food or anything, but my appetite’s definitely been off.\n\nMost days I’ll realize I’ve kind of just been grazing — like a few bites of something here and there — and then by supper I either don’t feel like dealing with making anything or I’ll just skip it because nothing sounds good. It’s weird because I’ll still know I should eat, but it feels like another chore, almost.\n\nAnd I have noticed my belt’s a little looser. I’m not trying to lose weight, so that caught my attention a bit. I haven’t been weighing myself constantly or anything, but clothes are fitting a little different, enough that I’ve noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s weird because I’ll still know I should eat, but it feels like another chore, almost.\n\nAnd I have noticed my belt’s a little looser. I’m not trying to lose weight, so that caught my attention a bit. I haven’t been weighing myself constantly or anything, but clothes are fitting a little different, enough that I’ve noticed.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been moving or speaking noticeably slower than usual lately?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / computer_systems_analyst / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed, anyway.

I mean, I feel slowed down in the sense that getting myself to do things takes more effort, but I don’t know if I’m literally moving slower. Nobody’s said anything to me like, “Why are you talking so slow?” or “Are you okay?” And I haven’t noticed myself dragging my feet around the office or anything obvious like that.

Speaking-wise, I think I’m pretty much the same. Maybe I pause more because I’m tired or trying to gather my thoughts, but I wouldn’t say I’m talking noticeably slower. It’s more like everything feels heavier internally than it probably looks from the outside.

I mean, outside of work it doesn’t just magically turn off when I close the laptop or leave the office. Like everything is still happening, I’m still there, I’m still doing the basic things, but there’s this dullness over it. Even when nothing specific is wrong in that exact moment, I still feel weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think there was a pretty clear turning point. It was about a month ago, maybe a little over or under, when I got asked to cover the senior analyst duties.\n\nAt first I tried to tell myself it was just temporary and I should be able to handle it, but that’s when I really started noticing this whole up-and-down pattern more. Like before that, I’d have stressful days, sure, but it didn’t feel as… noticeable or consuming. Once I was put in that role, it felt like everything started getting louder in my head, especially the pressure and the second-guessing.\n\nDoctor: If you were no longer covering the senior analyst duties, do you think this heavy low mood would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would mostly go away, no. I mean, I do think the senior analyst stuff is making it louder, definitely. It gives my brain a lot to latch onto. But the low feeling has sort of spread past just that.\n\nLike, even when I’m not actively dealing with work, I still have this heaviness most days lately. I’ll be at home doing normal things, or just trying to get through the evening, and it’s like everything is muted. That’s the best way I can put it — like the color’s been turned down. So if I wasn’t covering those duties, maybe I’d feel less pressure, but I don’t think I’d suddenly feel fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this low most days for at least the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It’s been long enough that it feels kind of embarrassing that I didn’t notice it clearly sooner, but not that long.\n\nLooking back, I’d say these low periods — and especially the guilty feeling that comes with them — have been around off and on for maybe about eleven months. It hasn’t been one perfectly straight line every single day for eleven months, but when I think back, that pattern has been there for most of the past year. It’s only gotten more obvious recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things much less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s actually one of the things that’s been bothering me, because I’m usually pretty simple that way. Like, I don’t need some big exciting thing to look forward to. Normally, if it’s the weekend, I’ll think about grilling something, or maybe going fishing if the weather’s decent, or just putting on a record and messing around in the garage or the house.\n\nBut lately I’ll have the stuff right there — the grill, the fishing gear, my records — and I just kind of… leave it. I’ll think, “I should do that,” because I know I usually like it, but then I don’t really care enough to start. It’s not even that I’m choosing something better instead. It’s more like the interest just isn’t there the way it normally is.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying this out loud, but yes, sort of. I haven’t made any kind of plan to hurt myself, and I don’t have some specific thing I’m intending to do. It’s not like I’m sitting there working out details or anything like that.\n\nBut I do catch myself having thoughts like… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up one morning. Not in a dramatic way, exactly. More like I’m so tired of feeling heavy and guilty and worn down that the idea of not having to keep doing it crosses my mind. Then I feel bad for even thinking that, because I know how that sounds.\n\nSo… no plan, and I’m not trying to hurt myself, but those thoughts about not waking up have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep has been kind of a mess lately. Not every single night in the exact same way, but enough that I notice it.\n\nSome mornings I wake up way too early — like before I actually need to be up — and my brain just starts going straight into work stuff. Client tickets, things I didn’t finish, little problems I need to troubleshoot… and once that starts, I can’t really get back to sleep. I’ll just lie there turning it over in my head, even if there’s nothing I can actually do about it at that hour.\n\nOther times, I technically sleep enough hours, or at least it looks that way if I count them, but I still wake up feeling worn out. Like the sleep didn’t really do what it was supposed to do. It’s frustrating, because I can’t always point to one thing and say, “That’s why I’m tired,” but I feel drained anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping track of it or anything. If I’m tired or stressed, then yeah, I might have to reread something or take a minute to get started, but I can’t say for sure that it’s been a major thing lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yeah, I think that’s actually a big part of why I came in.

I don’t know if I would have used the word “worthless” right away, because that sounds so extreme, but… I’ve been beating myself up a lot. Like, really getting stuck on this idea that I’m not actually senior-level material at work, that I somehow got people to believe I could handle more responsibility than I really can.

It’s like this running thought in the back of my head: “You fooled them. They’re going to figure it out. You’re not as capable as they think you are.” And then every mistake or delay feels like proof. Even if, objectively, maybe it’s just normal work stuff, my brain turns it into this whole indictment of me as a person.

So… yeah. Maybe not worthless every minute, but I’ve definitely been feeling like I’m not good enough, like I don’t deserve the role I’m in, and like I’ve let people think I’m more competent than I really am.

I keep thinking I’m not really “senior” material, like I somehow talked my way into people believing I can handle more responsibility than I actually can.\n\nAnd it’s not just a passing thought, you know? I’ll sit there replaying things I said in meetings or decisions I made, and I end up feeling like I fooled everyone. Like they’re going to realize I’m not as capable as they thought. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it’s been weighing on me enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone instead of just keeping it in my head.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you also feel down most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, outside of work it doesn’t just magically turn off when I close the laptop or leave the office. Most days lately there’s this kind of heavy, low feeling that follows me around.\n\nIt’s hard to describe exactly. It’s not always like I’m actively crying or anything that obvious. It’s more like I’m moving through the day at work and then at home with the color turned down, if that makes sense. Like everything is still happening, I’m still there, I’m still doing the basic things, but there’s this dullness over it. Even when nothing specific is wrong in that exact moment, I still feel weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think there was a pretty clear turning point. It was about a month ago, maybe a little over or under, when I got asked to cover the senior analyst duties.\n\nAt first I tried to tell myself it was just temporary and I should be able to handle it, but that’s when I really started noticing this whole up-and-down pattern more. Like before that, I’d have stressful days, sure, but it didn’t feel as… noticeable or consuming. Once I was put in that role, it felt like everything started getting louder in my head, especially the pressure and the second-guessing.\n\nDoctor: If you were no longer covering the senior analyst duties, do you think this heavy low mood would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would mostly go away, no. I mean, I do think the senior analyst stuff is making it louder, definitely. It gives my brain a lot to latch onto. But the low feeling has sort of spread past just that.\n\nLike, even when I’m not actively dealing with work, I still have this heaviness most days lately. I’ll be at home doing normal things, or just trying to get through the evening, and it’s like everything is muted. That’s the best way I can put it — like the color’s been turned down. So if I wasn’t covering those duties, maybe I’d feel less pressure, but I don’t think I’d suddenly feel fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this low most days for at least the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It’s been long enough that it feels kind of embarrassing that I didn’t notice it clearly sooner, but not that long.\n\nLooking back, I’d say these low periods — and especially the guilty feeling that comes with them — have been around off and on for maybe about eleven months. It hasn’t been one perfectly straight line every single day for eleven months, but when I think back, that pattern has been there for most of the past year. It’s only gotten more obvious recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things much less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s actually one of the things that’s been bothering me, because I’m usually pretty simple that way. Like, I don’t need some big exciting thing to look forward to. Normally, if it’s the weekend, I’ll think about grilling something, or maybe going fishing if the weather’s decent, or just putting on a record and messing around in the garage or the house.\n\nBut lately I’ll have the stuff right there — the grill, the fishing gear, my records — and I just kind of… leave it. I’ll think, “I should do that,” because I know I usually like it, but then I don’t really care enough to start. It’s not even that I’m choosing something better instead. It’s more like the interest just isn’t there the way it normally is.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying this out loud, but yes, sort of. I haven’t made any kind of plan to hurt myself, and I don’t have some specific thing I’m intending to do. It’s not like I’m sitting there working out details or anything like that.\n\nBut I do catch myself having thoughts like… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up one morning. Not in a dramatic way, exactly. More like I’m so tired of feeling heavy and guilty and worn down that the idea of not having to keep doing it crosses my mind. Then I feel bad for even thinking that, because I know how that sounds.\n\nSo… no plan, and I’m not trying to hurt myself, but those thoughts about not waking up have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep has been kind of a mess lately. Not every single night in the exact same way, but enough that I notice it.\n\nSome mornings I wake up way too early — like before I actually need to be up — and my brain just starts going straight into work stuff. Client tickets, things I didn’t finish, little problems I need to troubleshoot… and once that starts, I can’t really get back to sleep. I’ll just lie there turning it over in my head, even if there’s nothing I can actually do about it at that hour.\n\nOther times, I technically sleep enough hours, or at least it looks that way if I count them, but I still wake up feeling worn out. Like the sleep didn’t really do what it was supposed to do. It’s frustrating, because I can’t always point to one thing and say, “That’s why I’m tired,” but I feel drained anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean… I haven’t noticed it in the same clear way as some of the other stuff. With concentration, it’s a little hard for me to separate normal distraction from an actual problem, because my job kind of forces me to jump between things anyway — emails, tickets, someone asking a question, then back to whatever I was doing.\n\nThere are times I feel like I’m not as sharp as I’d like, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m having real trouble concentrating. I haven’t been keeping track of it or anything. If I’m tired or stressed, then yeah, I might have to reread something or take a minute to get started, but I can’t say for sure that it’s been a major thing lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like I’m collapsing or anything, but I feel worn down in this really annoying, everyday kind of way.\n\nBy the time I get home from the office, I’m just… done. And I don’t mean after doing anything especially intense, just a normal workday. I’ll get home and even stupid little things feel bigger than they should. Like checking the mail, or loading the dishwasher, or dealing with something that would normally take five minutes — I’ll look at it and feel like it’s this whole project.\n\nIt makes me feel kind of ridiculous, honestly, because I know those aren’t hard tasks. But my body and my brain both feel like they’ve already used up whatever energy I had for the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things, not just work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “a lot of different things,” exactly. Work is definitely the thing that grabs my brain the most, just because it’s always there and there’s always something unresolved.\n\nOutside of that… I mean, I worry about normal stuff, I guess. Bills, the house, little family things, whether I forgot to do something, that kind of everyday background noise. But I’m not sure it feels excessive across the board. It’s more like when I’m already worn out, even small things can feel louder than they should.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is maybe, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as worrying about everything. With concentration, it’s a little hard for me to separate normal distraction from an actual problem, because my job kind of forces me to jump between things anyway — emails, tickets, someone asking a question, then back to whatever I was doing.\n\nThere are times I feel like I’m not as sharp as I’d like, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m having real trouble concentrating. I haven’t been keeping track of it or anything. If I’m tired or stressed, then yeah, I might have to reread something or take a minute to get started, but I can’t say for sure that it’s been a major thing lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like I’m collapsing or anything, but I feel worn down in this really annoying, everyday kind of way.\n\nBy the time I get home from the office, I’m just… done. And I don’t mean after doing anything especially intense, just a normal workday. I’ll get home and even stupid little things feel bigger than they should. Like checking the mail, or loading the dishwasher, or dealing with something that would normally take five minutes — I’ll look at it and feel like it’s this whole project.\n\nIt makes me feel kind of ridiculous, honestly, because I know those aren’t hard tasks. But my body and my brain both feel like they’ve already used up whatever energy I had for the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things, not just work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “a lot of different things,” exactly. Work is definitely the thing that grabs my brain the most, just because it’s always there and there’s always something unresolved.\n\nOutside of that… I mean, I worry about normal stuff, I guess. Bills, the house, little family things, whether I forgot to do something, that kind of everyday background noise. But I’m not sure it feels excessive across the board. It’s more like when I’m already worn out, even small things can feel louder than they should.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is maybe, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as worrying about everything. It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of disasters all day. It’s more that certain things get stuck in my head, mostly work-related, and then the rest depends on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been eating noticeably less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Once I was put in that role, it felt like everything started getting louder in my head, especially the pressure and the second-guessing.\n\nDoctor: If you were no longer covering the senior analyst duties, do you think this heavy low mood would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would mostly go away, no. I mean, I do think the senior analyst stuff is making it louder, definitely. It gives my brain a lot to latch onto. I’ll be at home doing normal things, or just trying to get through the evening, and it’s like everything is muted. That’s the best way I can put it — like the color’s been turned down. So if I wasn’t covering those duties, maybe I’d feel less pressure, but I don’t think I’d suddenly feel fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this low most days for at least the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It’s been long enough that it feels kind of embarrassing that I didn’t notice it clearly sooner, but not that long.\n\nLooking back, I’d say these low periods — and especially the guilty feeling that comes with them — have been around off and on for maybe about eleven months. It hasn’t been one perfectly straight line every single day for eleven months, but when I think back, that pattern has been there for most of the past year. It’s only gotten more obvious recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things much less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s actually one of the things that’s been bothering me, because I’m usually pretty simple that way. Like, I don’t need some big exciting thing to look forward to. Normally, if it’s the weekend, I’ll think about grilling something, or maybe going fishing if the weather’s decent, or just putting on a record and messing around in the garage or the house.\n\nBut lately I’ll have the stuff right there — the grill, the fishing gear, my records — and I just kind of… leave it. It’s more like the interest just isn’t there the way it normally is.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying this out loud, but yes, sort of. I haven’t made any kind of plan to hurt myself, and I don’t have some specific thing I’m intending to do. It’s not like I’m sitting there working out details or anything like that.\n\nBut I do catch myself having thoughts like… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up one morning. Not in a dramatic way, exactly. More like I’m so tired of feeling heavy and guilty and worn down that the idea of not having to keep doing it crosses my mind. Then I feel bad for even thinking that, because I know how that sounds.\n\nSo… no plan, and I’m not trying to hurt myself, but those thoughts about not waking up have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep has been kind of a mess lately. Not every single night in the exact same way, but enough that I notice it.\n\nSome mornings I wake up way too early — like before I actually need to be up — and my brain just starts going straight into work stuff. Client tickets, things I didn’t finish, little problems I need to troubleshoot… and once that starts, I can’t really get back to sleep. I’ll just lie there turning it over in my head, even if there’s nothing I can actually do about it at that hour.\n\nOther times, I technically sleep enough hours, or at least it looks that way if I count them, but I still wake up feeling worn out. Like the sleep didn’t really do what it was supposed to do. It’s frustrating, because I can’t always point to one thing and say, “That’s why I’m tired,” but I feel drained anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean… I haven’t noticed it in the same clear way as some of the other stuff. With concentration, it’s a little hard for me to separate normal distraction from an actual problem, because my job kind of forces me to jump between things anyway — emails, tickets, someone asking a question, then back to whatever I was doing.\n\nThere are times I feel like I’m not as sharp as I’d like, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m having real trouble concentrating. I haven’t been keeping track of it or anything. If I’m tired or stressed, then yeah, I might have to reread something or take a minute to get started, but I can’t say for sure that it’s been a major thing lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like I’m collapsing or anything, but I feel worn down in this really annoying, everyday kind of way.\n\nBy the time I get home from the office, I’m just… done. And I don’t mean after doing anything especially intense, just a normal workday. I’ll get home and even stupid little things feel bigger than they should. Like checking the mail, or loading the dishwasher, or dealing with something that would normally take five minutes — I’ll look at it and feel like it’s this whole project.\n\nIt makes me feel kind of ridiculous, honestly, because I know those aren’t hard tasks. But my body and my brain both feel like they’ve already used up whatever energy I had for the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things, not just work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “a lot of different things,” exactly. Work is definitely the thing that grabs my brain the most, just because it’s always there and there’s always something unresolved.\n\nOutside of that… I mean, I worry about normal stuff, I guess. Bills, the house, little family things, whether I forgot to do something, that kind of everyday background noise. But I’m not sure it feels excessive across the board. It’s more like when I’m already worn out, even small things can feel louder than they should.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is maybe, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as worrying about everything. It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of disasters all day. It’s more that certain things get stuck in my head, mostly work-related, and then the rest depends on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been eating noticeably less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There might be moments where I’m distracted or I’m talking to someone and I seem okay, but underneath it, it’s like the low feeling doesn’t really lift.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period when you felt unusually energized or unusually up, much more than your normal self?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / computer_systems_analyst / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Before this low stretch, I had several days where I felt almost too sure of myself and would snap at people over small stuff, like a slow email or a client asking the same question twice.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m a little hesitant to call it “up,” because it didn’t feel like I was walking around happy or anything like that. But before this low stretch, there were several days where I felt… almost too sure of myself, if that makes sense.

Like, I had this feeling that I knew exactly what needed to happen and everyone else was kind of in my way. At work especially, I remember getting really irritated over things that normally might annoy me but wouldn’t set me off that much—like if someone took too long to respond to an email, or a client asked the same question twice. I’d snap a little, or I’d feel this sharp anger right away, like, “Why are we wasting time on this?” And in the moment it felt justified, but looking back it seems kind of out of proportion.

It wasn’t just confidence like a good day. It was more like I was overly certain of myself and much less patient than I usually am. So yes, I guess there was a period like that, just several days, before I crashed into this more depressed feeling.

  {

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.9, F43.2, F41.1, F60.6, Z56.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been really stuck in this loop of beating myself up. It’s mostly about work, or at least that’s where it shows up the loudest. I keep thinking I’m not really “senior” material, like I somehow talked my way into people believing I can handle more responsibility than I actually can.\n\nAnd it’s not just a passing thought, you know? I’ll sit there replaying things I said in meetings or decisions I made, and I end up feeling like I fooled everyone. Like they’re going to realize I’m not as capable as they thought. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it’s been weighing on me enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone instead of just keeping it in my head.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you also feel down most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, outside of work it doesn’t just magically turn off when I close the laptop or leave the office. Most days lately there’s this kind of heavy, low feeling that follows me around.\n\nIt’s hard to describe exactly. It’s not always like I’m actively crying or anything that obvious. It’s more like I’m moving through the day at work and then at home with the color turned down, if that makes sense. Like everything is still happening, I’m still there, I’m still doing the basic things, but there’s this dullness over it. Even when nothing specific is wrong in that exact moment, I still feel weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think there was a pretty clear turning point. It was about a month ago, maybe a little over or under, when I got asked to cover the senior analyst duties.\n\nAt first I tried to tell myself it was just temporary and I should be able to handle it, but that’s when I really started noticing this whole up-and-down pattern more. Like before that, I’d have stressful days, sure, but it didn’t feel as… noticeable or consuming. Once I was put in that role, it felt like everything started getting louder in my head, especially the pressure and the second-guessing.\n\nDoctor: If you were no longer covering the senior analyst duties, do you think this heavy low mood would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would mostly go away, no. I mean, I do think the senior analyst stuff is making it louder, definitely. It gives my brain a lot to latch onto. But the low feeling has sort of spread past just that.\n\nLike, even when I’m not actively dealing with work, I still have this heaviness most days lately. I’ll be at home doing normal things, or just trying to get through the evening, and it’s like everything is muted. That’s the best way I can put it — like the color’s been turned down. So if I wasn’t covering those duties, maybe I’d feel less pressure, but I don’t think I’d suddenly feel fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this low most days for at least the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It’s been long enough that it feels kind of embarrassing that I didn’t notice it clearly sooner, but not that long.\n\nLooking back, I’d say these low periods — and especially the guilty feeling that comes with them — have been around off and on for maybe about eleven months. It hasn’t been one perfectly straight line every single day for eleven months, but when I think back, that pattern has been there for most of the past year. It’s only gotten more obvious recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things much less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s actually one of the things that’s been bothering me, because I’m usually pretty simple that way. Like, I don’t need some big exciting thing to look forward to. Normally, if it’s the weekend, I’ll think about grilling something, or maybe going fishing if the weather’s decent, or just putting on a record and messing around in the garage or the house.\n\nBut lately I’ll have the stuff right there — the grill, the fishing gear, my records — and I just kind of… leave it. I’ll think, “I should do that,” because I know I usually like it, but then I don’t really care enough to start. It’s not even that I’m choosing something better instead. It’s more like the interest just isn’t there the way it normally is.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying this out loud, but yes, sort of. I haven’t made any kind of plan to hurt myself, and I don’t have some specific thing I’m intending to do. It’s not like I’m sitting there working out details or anything like that.\n\nBut I do catch myself having thoughts like… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up one morning. Not in a dramatic way, exactly. More like I’m so tired of feeling heavy and guilty and worn down that the idea of not having to keep doing it crosses my mind. Then I feel bad for even thinking that, because I know how that sounds.\n\nSo… no plan, and I’m not trying to hurt myself, but those thoughts about not waking up have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep has been kind of a mess lately. Not every single night in the exact same way, but enough that I notice it.\n\nSome mornings I wake up way too early — like before I actually need to be up — and my brain just starts going straight into work stuff. Client tickets, things I didn’t finish, little problems I need to troubleshoot… and once that starts, I can’t really get back to sleep. I’ll just lie there turning it over in my head, even if there’s nothing I can actually do about it at that hour.\n\nOther times, I technically sleep enough hours, or at least it looks that way if I count them, but I still wake up feeling worn out. Like the sleep didn’t really do what it was supposed to do. It’s frustrating, because I can’t always point to one thing and say, “That’s why I’m tired,” but I feel drained anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean… I haven’t noticed it in the same clear way as some of the other stuff. With concentration, it’s a little hard for me to separate normal distraction from an actual problem, because my job kind of forces me to jump between things anyway — emails, tickets, someone asking a question, then back to whatever I was doing.\n\nThere are times I feel like I’m not as sharp as I’d like, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m having real trouble concentrating. I haven’t been keeping track of it or anything. If I’m tired or stressed, then yeah, I might have to reread something or take a minute to get started, but I can’t say for sure that it’s been a major thing lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like I’m collapsing or anything, but I feel worn down in this really annoying, everyday kind of way.\n\nBy the time I get home from the office, I’m just… done. And I don’t mean after doing anything especially intense, just a normal workday. I’ll get home and even stupid little things feel bigger than they should. Like checking the mail, or loading the dishwasher, or dealing with something that would normally take five minutes — I’ll look at it and feel like it’s this whole project.\n\nIt makes me feel kind of ridiculous, honestly, because I know those aren’t hard tasks. But my body and my brain both feel like they’ve already used up whatever energy I had for the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things, not just work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “a lot of different things,” exactly. Work is definitely the thing that grabs my brain the most, just because it’s always there and there’s always something unresolved.\n\nOutside of that… I mean, I worry about normal stuff, I guess. Bills, the house, little family things, whether I forgot to do something, that kind of everyday background noise. But I’m not sure it feels excessive across the board. It’s more like when I’m already worn out, even small things can feel louder than they should.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is maybe, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as worrying about everything. It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of disasters all day. It’s more that certain things get stuck in my head, mostly work-related, and then the rest depends on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been eating noticeably less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic way, like I’m not refusing food or anything, but my appetite’s definitely been off.\n\nMost days I’ll realize I’ve kind of just been grazing — like a few bites of something here and there — and then by supper I either don’t feel like dealing with making anything or I’ll just skip it because nothing sounds good. It’s weird because I’ll still know I should eat, but it feels like another chore, almost.\n\nAnd I have noticed my belt’s a little looser. I’m not trying to lose weight, so that caught my attention a bit. I haven’t been weighing myself constantly or anything, but clothes are fitting a little different, enough that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably slower than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed, anyway.\n\nI mean, I feel slowed down in the sense that getting myself to do things takes more effort, but I don’t know if I’m literally moving slower. Nobody’s said anything to me like, “Why are you talking so slow?” or “Are you okay?” And I haven’t noticed myself dragging my feet around the office or anything obvious like that.\n\nSpeaking-wise, I think I’m pretty much the same. Maybe I pause more because I’m tired or trying to gather my thoughts, but I wouldn’t say I’m talking noticeably slower. It’s more like everything feels heavier internally than it probably looks from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think that’s actually a big part of why I came in.\n\nI don’t know if I would have used the word “worthless” right away, because that sounds so extreme, but… I’ve been beating myself up a lot. Like, really getting stuck on this idea that I’m not actually senior-level material at work, that I somehow got people to believe I could handle more responsibility than I really can.\n\nIt’s like this running thought in the back of my head: “You fooled them. They’re going to figure it out. You’re not as capable as they think you are.” And then every mistake or delay feels like proof. Even if, objectively, maybe it’s just normal work stuff, my brain turns it into this whole indictment of me as a person.\n\nSo… yeah. Maybe not worthless every minute, but I’ve definitely been feeling like I’m not good enough, like I don’t deserve the role I’m in, and like I’ve let people think I’m more competent than I really am.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a period of at least two weeks when you felt this low most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’d say it’s been at least that long, maybe longer, where most days I wake up and there’s just this heavy, low feeling there already.\n\nIt’s not like I’m crying all day or completely unable to function. I still go to work, I still get through the basic things, but it feels like I’m doing all of it with the color turned down, if that makes sense. Like I’m present, but everything feels flat and harder than it should. Work, coming home, even just sitting around afterward… it all has this kind of gray weight to it.\n\nAnd it’s been pretty much most of the day, most days lately. There might be moments where I’m distracted or I’m talking to someone and I seem okay, but underneath it, it’s like the low feeling doesn’t really lift.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energized or unusually up, much more than your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m a little hesitant to call it “up,” because it didn’t feel like I was walking around happy or anything like that. But before this low stretch, there were several days where I felt… almost too sure of myself, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I had this feeling that I knew exactly what needed to happen and everyone else was kind of in my way. At work especially, I remember getting really irritated over things that normally might annoy me but wouldn’t set me off that much—like if someone took too long to respond to an email, or a client asked the same question twice. I’d snap a little, or I’d feel this sharp anger right away, like, “Why are we wasting time on this?” And in the moment it felt justified, but looking back it seems kind of out of proportion.\n\nIt wasn’t just confidence like a good day. It was more like I was overly certain of myself and much less patient than I usually am. So yes, I guess there was a period like that, just several days, before I crashed into this more depressed feeling.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, that was one of the stranger parts, actually.

Normally if I only sleep three or four hours, I’m useless the next day. Like, I’m foggy, I’m dragging myself around, I need coffee just to function. But during that stretch, I’d go to bed late or wake up really early, and I’d only have gotten maybe three or four hours of sleep, and somehow I’d get up feeling like, okay, let’s go, I’ve got things to do.

It didn’t feel normal for me. It wasn’t like I was rested in the usual way, exactly, but I didn’t feel tired the way I should have. I remember thinking it was odd, but at the time I kind of treated it like a good thing, like maybe I was just being productive or had momentum. Looking back, though, yeah… needing that little sleep and still feeling ready to tackle the day was not typical for me.

It was more like I was overly certain of myself and much less patient than I usually am. I’d snap a little, or I’d feel this sharp anger right away, like, “Why are we wasting time on this?” And in the moment it felt justified, but looking back it seems kind of out of proportion.\n\nIt wasn’t just confidence like a good day. It was more like I was overly certain of myself and much less patient than I usually am. So yes, I guess there was a period like that, just several days, before I crashed into this more depressed feeling.\n\nDoctor: During that several-day period before the crash, did you need much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that was one of the stranger parts, actually.\n\nNormally if I only sleep three or four hours, I’m useless the next day. Like, I’m foggy, I’m dragging myself around, I need coffee just to function. But during that stretch, I’d go to bed late or wake up really early, and I’d only have gotten maybe three or four hours of sleep, and somehow I’d get up feeling like, okay, let’s go, I’ve got things to do.\n\nIt didn’t feel normal for me. It wasn’t like I was rested in the usual way, exactly, but I didn’t feel tired the way I should have. I remember thinking it was odd, but at the time I kind of treated it like a good thing, like maybe I was just being productive or had momentum. Looking back, though, yeah… needing that little sleep and still feeling ready to tackle the day was not typical for me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring that several-day period before the crash, did your behavior cause any problems at work or with other people?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / computer_systems_analyst / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m still making it to work, but I’ve had to double-check myself more and I’ve pulled back from friends and family because I don’t trust my mood or my judgment lately.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… not like I got fired or anything, and I’m still showing up to work, still doing the job, but it did cause some problems, or at least it made things harder than they needed to be.

I noticed I had to double-check myself a lot more. Like before sending an email, I’d reread it and think, “Is this too sharp? Am I being rude here?” Or after a meeting I’d replay what I said and wonder if I came across as irritated or too blunt. I’m a computer systems analyst, so a lot of what I do is detail-oriented anyway, but this felt different. It wasn’t just checking the technical side of things—it was checking my tone, my decisions, whether I was reacting too fast.

With people outside work, I’ve kind of pulled back. Friends, family… I haven’t been reaching out as much, and if someone texts me I might wait longer to answer because I don’t really trust what mood I’m in. I don’t want to say something snappy or make some decision based on how I feel in that moment. So I’ve been keeping to myself more, which probably isn’t great either, but it feels safer than risking saying or doing the wrong thing.
